[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ================
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> = doomgeneric =
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ================
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> To port DOOM easly, I made some changes to source code.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Now, all you need to do is implement the functions in doomgeneric.h to port DOOM to a new system.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> doomgeneric_win.c is a Windows GDI port. It is also a good example for how to port doomgeneric.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Original readme
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ===============
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Here it is, at long last.  The DOOM source code is released for your
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> non-profit use.  You still need real DOOM data to work with this code.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> If you don't actually own a real copy of one of the DOOMs, you should
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> still be able to find them at software stores.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Many thanks to Bernd Kreimeier for taking the time to clean up the
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> project and make sure that it actually works.  Projects tends to rot if
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> you leave it alone for a few years, and it takes effort for someone to
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> deal with it again.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> The bad news:  this code only compiles and runs on linux.  We couldn't
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> release the dos code because of a copyrighted sound library we used
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> (wow, was that a mistake -- I write my own sound code now), and I
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> honestly don't even know what happened to the port that microsoft did
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> to windows.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Still, the code is quite portable, and it should be straightforward to
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> bring it up on just about any platform.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> I wrote this code a long, long time ago, and there are plenty of things
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> that seem downright silly in retrospect (using polar coordinates for
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> clipping comes to mind), but overall it should still be a usefull base
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> to experiment and build on.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> The basic rendering concept -- horizontal and vertical lines of constant
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Z with fixed light shading per band was dead-on, but the implementation
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> could be improved dramatically from the original code if it were
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> revisited.  The way the rendering proceded from walls to floors to
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> sprites could be collapsed into a single front-to-back walk of the bsp
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> tree to collect information, then draw all the contents of a subsector
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> on the way back up the tree.  It requires treating floors and ceilings
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> as polygons, rather than just the gaps between walls, and it requires
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> clipping sprite billboards into subsector fragments, but it would be
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> The Right Thing.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> The movement and line of sight checking against the lines is one of the
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> bigger misses that I look back on.  It is messy code that had some
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> failure cases, and there was a vastly simpler (and faster) solution
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> sitting in front of my face.  I used the BSP tree for rendering things,
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> but I didn't realize at the time that it could also be used for
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> environment testing.  Replacing the line of sight test with a bsp line
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> clip would be pretty easy.  Sweeping volumes for movement gets a bit
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> tougher, and touches on many of the challenges faced in quake / quake2
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> with edge bevels on polyhedrons.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Some project ideas:
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Port it to your favorite operating system.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Add some rendering features -- transparency, look up / down, slopes,
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> etc.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Add some game features -- weapons, jumping, ducking, flying, etc.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Create a packet server based internet game.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Create a client / server based internet game.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Do a 3D accelerated version.  On modern hardware (fast pentium + 3DFX)
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> you probably wouldn't even need to be clever -- you could just draw the
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> entire level and get reasonable speed.  With a touch of effort, it should
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> easily lock at 60 fps (well, there are some issues with DOOM's 35 hz
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> timebase...).  The biggest issues would probably be the non-power of two
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> texture sizes and the walls composed of multiple textures.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> I don't have a real good guess at how many people are going to be
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> playing with this, but if significant projects are undertaken, it would
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> be cool to see a level of community cooperation.  I know that most early
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> projects are going to be rough hacks done in isolation, but I would be
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> very pleased to see a coordinated 'net release of an improved, backwards
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> compatable version of DOOM on multiple platforms next year.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Have fun.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> John Carmack
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 12-23-97
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> =====
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> # doomgeneric
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> The purpose of doomgeneric is to make porting Doom easier.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Of course Doom is already portable but with doomgeneric it is possible with just a few functions.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> To try it you will need a WAD file (game data). If you don't own the game, shareware version is freely available (doom1.wad).
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> # porting
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Create a file named doomgeneric_yourplatform.c and just implement these functions to suit your platform.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> * DG_Init
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> * DG_DrawFrame
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> * DG_SleepMs
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> * DG_GetTicksMs
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> * DG_GetKey
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |Functions            |Description|
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |---------------------|-----------|
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_Init              |Initialize your platfrom (create window, framebuffer, etc...).
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_DrawFrame         |Frame is ready in DG_ScreenBuffer. Copy it to your platform's screen.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_SleepMs           |Sleep in milliseconds.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_GetTicksMs        |The ticks passed since launch in milliseconds.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_GetKey            |Provide keyboard events.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> |DG_SetWindowTitle    |Not required. This is for setting the window title as Doom sets this from WAD file.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ### main loop
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> At start, call doomgeneric_Create().
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> In a loop, call doomgeneric_Tick().
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> In simplest form:
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ```
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> int main(int argc, char **argv)
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> {
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     doomgeneric_Create(argc, argv);
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     while (1)
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     {
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->         doomgeneric_Tick();
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     }
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->     return 0;
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> }
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ```
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> # sound
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Sound is much harder to implement! If you need sound, take a look at SDL port. It fully supports sound and music! Where to start? Define FEATURE_SOUND, assign DG_sound_module and DG_music_module.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> # platforms
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Ported platforms include Windows, X11, SDL, emscripten. Just look at (doomgeneric_win.c, doomgeneric_xlib.c, doomgeneric_sdl.c).
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> Makefiles provided for each platform.
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> ## emscripten
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> You can try it directly here:
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> https://ozkl.github.io/doomgeneric/
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> 
[tool-0e8] exec_command -> emscripten port is based on SDL port, so it supports sound and music! For music, timidity backend is used.
[tool-0e8] exec_command ->

[tool-444] exec_command ->     
[tool-444] exec_command ->     if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = parent;
[tool-444] exec_command ->     return current;
[tool-444] exec_command -> }

[tool-fc5] exec_command -> #if defined(ARCH_X86)
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> 
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> // Real syscall function for Linux x86_64
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> static long real_syscall6(long syscall_num, long arg1, long arg2, long arg3,
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->                      long arg4, long arg5, long arg6) {
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     long result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     /*
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * Use a simplified approach that directly specifies which
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * registers to use for each argument
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     __asm__ volatile(
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Input arguments are already in the right locations */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "syscall"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "=a" (result)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "a" (syscall_num),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "D" (arg1),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "S" (arg2),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "d" (arg3),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg4)   /* r10 */,
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg5)   /* r8 */,
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg6)   /* r9 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "rcx", "r11", "memory"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     );
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     /* Check for error and set errno */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     if (result < 0 && result > -4096) {
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         errno = -result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         return -1;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     }
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     return result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> 
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> }
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> 
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> 
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> #else
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> // Real syscall function for MIPS
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> static long real_syscall6(long syscall_num, long arg1, long arg2, long arg3,
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->                      long arg4, long arg5, long arg6) {
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     long result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     /*
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * Hacky MIPS syscall approach:
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * - Syscall number goes in $v0 (register 2)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * - First four arguments go in $a0-$a3 (registers 4-7)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * - Additional arguments go in $t0-$t1 (registers 8-9)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * - Result comes back in $v0 (register 2)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     __asm__ volatile(
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Load syscall number into $v0 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $v0, %1\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Load first four arguments into $a0-$a3 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $a0, %2\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $a1, %3\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $a2, %4\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $a3, %5\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Load additional arguments into $t0-$t1 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $t0, %6\n\t"  /* 5th arg in $t0 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move $t1, %7\n\t"  /* 6th arg in $t1 */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Make the syscall */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "syscall\n\t"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Get the return value */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         "move %0, $v0"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Output parameter */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "=r" (result)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Input parameters */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "r" (syscall_num),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg1),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg2),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg3),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg4),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg5),
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->           "r" (arg6)
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         /* Clobbered registers */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         : "v0", "a0", "a1", "a2", "a3", "t0", "t1", "memory"
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     );
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     /* 
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * MIPS typically uses negative return values for errors
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      * and sets errno accordingly
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->      */
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     if (result < 0) {
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         errno = -result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->         return -1;
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     }
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->     return result;
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> }
[tool-fc5] exec_command -> #endif
[tool-fc5] exec_command ->

This is very informative. Key functions:

- `syscall6` at 0x43d974: This is the main syscall wrapper. Looking at the code:
```
syscall6(a0=syscall_num, a1=arg1, a2=arg2, a3=arg3, [t0..]) 
```
Wait, actually MIPS o32 syscall convention: syscall number in $v0, args in $a0-$a3, then $t0-$t1. Let's decode `syscall6`:
- v1 = a3, t2 = a2, t3 = a1, t4 = a0 (syscall num via move t4,a0 after li at,1; bne).
- If syscall num == 1 (SYS_write) AND arg1 (fd, t3) < 2 (fd 0 or 1), then do some special handling:
  - t4=1 (syscall write), t5=0
  - v0 = 1 (syscall num), a0 = fd, a1 = buf (t3->a0? wait: a0 = t3 = original arg1 which is... hmm), ...

Actually wait, let me re-decode. In syscall6:
- v1 = a3 (arg3)
- t2 = a2 (arg2)
- t3 = a1 (arg1)
- t4 = a0 (syscall number)
- if (a0 == 1) and (t3 < 2): special case - writes to fd 0/1. t4=1, t5=0, v0=t4=1, a0=t3 (fd), a1=t2 (buf?), a2=v1 (count)... wait t2 was a2 which is arg2. For write syscall convention, original args are (fd, buf, count) -> arg1=fd=t3, arg2=buf=t2, arg3=count=v1. So yes a0=fd, a1=buf, a2=count EB.
- Otherwise general: v0 = t4 (syscall num), a0 = t3, a1 = t2, a2 = v1, a3 = t5=0, t0=0, t1=0.

- result in v0 -> v1; if v1 < 0: at = -v1; errno at address 0x4b0a80 (= errno symbol?); v1 = -1; return v1.

So typical syscall handling: syscall instruction. In the interpreter, we need to handle the `syscall` instruction (opcode 0x0000000c). Since the VM is an interpreter, we handle special opcode → emulated syscall with v0 as syscall number and registers a0-a3, t0-t1 as args.

- `mkdir`: syscall 83.
- `exit`: syscall 60, then infinite loop `j 43dab4`. So after exit syscall succeeds, it jumps to itself. In the VM, when exit is called, we should halt the VM. The syscall 60 (exit) is caught in our syscall handler; we can call process.exit.

Note: stat/fstat/lstat are stubs that zero the buffer (no actual syscall) and return -1.

Also note: sys_write (syscall 1) special-cases fd 0/1? Actually the condition is t3 < 2 meaning fd==0 or fd==1. Interesting — maybe it does that to handle buffered stdio writes to stdout/stderr? Unclear, but it still makes syscall 1.

Wait, in MIPS, the instruction in the delay slot (the one after jal) is executed before the jump. Actually for `jal target`, the instruction at the address following the jal (in the delay slot) is executed before the branch. So `li a1,0` is executed before `doomgeneric_Create`? Hmm. Actually MIPS branch delay semantics: the instruction in the delay slot (immediately after a branch/jump) is executed regardless. For `jal 439560`, the `li a1,0` is in the delay slot, so a0=0 (li before), then a1=0, then jump to doomgeneric_Create with a0=0, a1=0. Yes since li a0,0 is before jal, and li a1,0 in delay slot.

Approach:
1. Write a MIPS32 interpreter in Node.js:
   - Load ELF32 little-endian MIPS.
   - Create memory: Buffer for the address space.
   - Map segments: .text (0x400110, size 0x3dbd0), .rodata (0x43dce0, size 0x18834), .data (0x466520, size 0xeb80), .bss (huge, zero-filled).
   - The loads: LOAD segment 2 at vaddr 0x400000 (filesz 0x56514); LOAD segment 3 at vaddr 0x466520 filesz 0xeb80 memsz 0x4004b270.
   - We can just allocate a single huge Buffer (or multiple) covering valid memory. Since .bss is 1GB, allocate ~1.07GB. Or allocate lazily: only allocate pages that are touched. Given .bss is huge and DOOM's malloc likely touches a large portion (it uses the whole arena for zone memory), we may need most of it. Let's check malloc implementation to know how much is used.

[tool-949] exec_command -> // ZONE MEMORY ALLOCATION
[tool-949] exec_command -> //
[tool-949] exec_command -> // There is never any space between memblocks,
[tool-949] exec_command -> //  and there will never be two contiguous free memblocks.
[tool-949] exec_command -> // The rover can be left pointing at a non-empty block.
[tool-949] exec_command -> //
[tool-949] exec_command -> // It is of no value to free a cachable block,
[tool-949] exec_command -> //  because it will get overwritten automatically if needed.
[tool-949] exec_command -> // 
[tool-949] exec_command ->

[tool-949] exec_command ->     
[tool-949] exec_command ->     // scan through the block list,
[tool-949] exec_command ->     // looking for the first free block

[tool-949] exec_command ->         }

Wait at 0x4399d4 `li t6,1024`, and each iteration adds ... Actually the loop at malloc+0x8c initializes 1024 free lists each of size... 0x400? Let me see: 

```
li t6,1024
...
addu t8,t5,t3   ; t5 = addr, t3 = size
sw t7,0(t5)     ; store at t5
move t7,t5
addiu t6,t6,-1
bnez t6,...      ; loop 1024 times
move t5,t8       ; t5 += size
```

And the sizes: `lb` at 0x404b178c is a flag `if (malloc_initialized_flag)`. Then `lw v0, 2836(at)` at 0x4b0b14 is `heap_end = 0x4b0b14` (initial 0). `t1 = 0x4b0b40` (2836+?)... hmm this is the basic heap start = 0x4b0b40. Actually `addiu t1,at,2880` = 0x4b0b40. The malloc init loop at 0x4399c8:
```
sll at,v1,0x2    ; at = v1*4 (index into sizes?)
sll t4,v1,0x4    ; t4 = v1*16
addu t5,t1,v0    ; t5 = 0x4b0b40 + heap_end
... 
addu at,a1,at    ; at = sizes + v1*4
lw at,0(at)      ; at = sizes[v1]
addiu at,at,7
and t3,at,a2     ; t3 = round up to 8
addu at,t0,t4    ; at = 0x404b174c + v1*16 (free_list[v1])
sw t5,4(at)      ; free_list[v1].start = t5
sw t3,8(at)      ; free_list[v1].size = t3
sw zero,12(at)   ; (count)=0
...
loop v1 0..3 (4 pools? bnez v1,t2 where t2=4)
```
So 4 size classes initialized, each 1024 blocks = 4096 blocks total. Then `subu t4,t8,t3` t4 = end. Wait after loop, `addiu v1,v1,1; bne v1,t2` loops 4 times. After it, heap_end = t8 offset...

So I_ZoneBase:
- s1 = 6 (default zone size in MB? `li s1,6`)
- a0 = s1 << 20 = 6 * 0x100000 = 6MB
- Loop: try allocate size = 6MB minus 0x100000 (s4=0xfff00000 → s5 = a0 + s4 = 6MB - 1MB = 5MB). Actually:
  - a0 = 6 << 20 = 0x600000 (6MB)
  - s4 = 0xfff00000 (i.e., -0x100000)
  - s5 = a0 + s4 = 0x500000 (5MB)
  - loop: call malloc(s5); if returns non-zero, break; else s2-- (s2=s1=6 initially?), s1... wait s2 = s1 = 6 at the start. First iteration: malloc(5MB). If it fails (v0==0), `beqz v0, 40e314` → loop: s2=s2-1 then a0=s1<<20 again? Hmm the loop increments? Let me re-read: At 0x40e308: a0 = s1 << 20. At 0x40e314: slt at,s2,s1. At 0x40e320: sw a0,0(s0) — store size. jal malloc with a0 = s5 = a0 + s4; in delay slot: s2 = s2 - 1. After return: beqz v0, 0x40e314 (loop back if failed). At top of loop: s2 = s1? No wait s2 was decremented. Actually, at 0x40e314 (loop start): `slt at,s2,s1; bnez at, 40e378` means if s2 < s1 then exit loop (only first iteration when s2==s1). Then continue alloc.

So it tries malloc(size - 1MB) where size starts at 6MB. Only one iteration essentially? Since after first attempt s2 becomes 5, and the top of loop checks s2 < s1 → 5<6 → exit loop. So it allocates 5MB. That malloc implementation probably doubles until success? Whatever — mainzone size = 5MB (0x500000). Good — so the 1GB .bss is not all allocated as "zone". Only 5MB is used for the zone. But .bss itself (1GB) is the static memory region that must exist. In our VM, we need a 1GB buffer to cover the .bss even if unused.

Actually, the malloc implementation initializes 4 pools of 1024 blocks each plus uses a large heap region for big allocations. Let me analyze malloc:

```
malloc:
 if size < 129: use small pool (sizes[4] classes) -- the sizes array is at .rodata 0x456504. 
 else: basic_malloc (big block allocator)
```

basic_malloc:
- round up size to 16, compute size class t0 via log2-ish.
- free_list table at 0x4b0a94 (free_lists), 31 entries.
- if no free block, extend heap: heap_end at 0x4b0b14 (initial 0), heap base 0x4b0b40.
- So big allocations come from the arena at 0x4b0b40 upward consequentiallytbally.

The 5MB zone is allocated in the arena around 0x4b0b40+... Let me see: first big allocation (5MB) would start at 0x4b0b40 + 8 (header at 0x4b0b40, payload at 0x4b0b48). Since .bss huge spans up to 0x4754b790 > 0x4b0b40, plenty of room. So .bss covers the entire arena.

So memory layout:
- .text: 0x400110 – 0x403ce0ish
- .rodata: 0x43dce0 – 0x456514
- .data: 0x466520 – 0x4750c0
- .bss: 0x4750c0 – 0x4754b790 (size 0x4003c6d0)

For the VM, allocate one big Buffer of size (0x4754b790 - something)... but the addresses include low addresses 0x400000. The LOAD segments cover from 0x400000. We can allocate a Buffer of size = end_of_bss - 0x400000 ≈ 0x4354b790 ≈ 1.07GB. Base = 0x400000. This is simpler: single Buffer mapping VA [0x400000, 0x4754b790).

Alternatively, lazy paging. But a plain Buffer is simpler. 1.07GB allocation is OK on this machine. However, note Node's Buffer.of(1.07GB) zero-fills — might take a few seconds. Acceptable.

But wait — do we really need the full 1GB? The malloc arena and zone need maybe tens of MB. But addresses like free_lists at 0x4b0a94, DG_ScreenBuffer at 0x4b0a40 — all within first ~1MB of .bss. Actually .bss starts at 0x4750c0暢. 0x4b0a94 - 0x4750c0 = 0x39bd4 ≈ 236KB. So all static variables are within a few hundred KB. But malloc's arena extends upward from 0x4b0b40 as big allocations are made. The biggest allocations: DG_ScreenBuffer = 640*400*4 = 1MB; the zone 5MB; textures/levels data, maybe tens of MB total. It's unlikely to reach 1GB. But to be safe and to faithfully emulate memory, allocating the full .bss is safest, since programs may reference beyond.

Alternatively, we could implement a sparse/lazy memory: allocate in pages and zero-fill on demandTmcc. That's more complex but avoids the 1GB upfront allocation. Given we have 127GB RAM, just allocating 1GB once is fine)Skip.

Let me estimate the performance. The DOOM game loop: it runs at 35 tics/sec with each tic doing a lot of work. But we only need the first frame. The boot process is heavy but might complete in reasonable time in a JS interpreter if optimized.

Actually wait — how much computation until first frame? Let's trace:
- __start → doomgeneric_Create(0,0):
  - M_FindResponseFile() — parses args; with argc=0, might do nothing.
  - DG_Init() — prints message => printf -> write syscall to stdout.
  - D_DoomMain() — initializes everything: WAD loading (reads doom.wad via fopen/fread — the fake_fs or real filesystem reads?), Z_Init, I_Init, D_DoomLoop probably. Wait, does D_DoomMain loop forever? In doomgeneric, D_DoomMain sets up and eventually calls D_DoomLoop which is a while(1) loop of ticks. Hmm, then doomgeneric_Create never returns Environmental. Let me check D_DoomMain in d_main.c.

Let me read d_main.c to understand boot process.

[tool-db3] exec_command ->         // for the options menu (says "Fullscreen:"). Why the perpetrators

[tool-db3] exec_command ->         // I don't know.
[tool-db3] exec_command ->         //

So D_DoomMain calls D_DoomLoop() which never returns (it's the main loop). Wait, but doomgeneric_Create calls D_DoomMain(); and D_DoomMain calls D_DoomLoop(); which runs forever. So __start's loop of calling doomgeneric_Tick is never reached? Actually D_DoomLoop itself calls `doomgeneric_Tick()` once at the end (per this custom source). So the whole game loop is inside D_DoomLoop... Wait, D_DoomLoop overrides the original Doom loop: this custom version initializes graphics, then calls doomgeneric_Tick() once and returns? Let's look at the code again:

```
void D_DoomLoop (void)
{
    ...
    doomgeneric_Tick();
}
```

It calls doomgeneric_Tick once and returns! That's the weird custom thing. Then D_DoomMain returns, doomgeneric_Create returns to __start, and __start's infinite loop calls doomgeneric_Tick over and over. Good. So the main loop is in __start.

OK so the boot process: D_DoomMain init → D_DoomLoop → doomgeneric_Tick once (renders first frame? maybe the title screen), then __start loop calls doomgeneric_Tick forever, each tick calls DG_DrawFrame which writes /tmp/frame.bmp every 10th.

Actually the first `doomgeneric_Tick` (inside D_DoomLoop) calls DG_DrawFrame with drawk=0 → writes /tmp/frame.bmp (first frame = title screen). Then the __start loop continues.

The first frame should be the title screen (D_StartTitle → D_AdvanceDemo → gamestate=GS_DEMOSCREEN, pagename=TITLEPIC, pagetic=170). After 170 tics, it advances to demo1. But the first tick draws the title.

So the goal: run VM until first DG_DrawFrame completes → verify /tmp/frame.bmp contains a valid BMP.

Now the heavy lifting: implement a MIPS interpreter fast enough and correct enough to boot DOOM.

Let me now consider what syscalls the program will use:
- write (1) to stdout/stderr for printf
- open (2), close (3), read (0), lseek (8) for reading doom.wad / configs / writing BMP
- mkdir (83)? maybe for savegame dir. In M_SetConfigDir, it might mkdir.
- rename (82), unlink (87)? maybe config writes.
- exit (60) — at exit.
- time/gettimeofday maybe.
- fstat/stat are stubs in the binary (they zero the buffer and return -1). But libc functions like fopen might need to stat? Let me check the fopen implementation in the binary. Actually my_stdlib provides its own fopen which probably uses open/read/write/close/lseek syscalls. The fake_fs.c defines an in-memory FS, but is fake_fs.c compiled into the binary? Let me check symbols for sys_open, sys_read, sys_write, etc. and which functions are actually used.

The fopen implementation directly does a syscall with v0 = 2 (SYS_open), a0 = path, a1 = flags (t3: 2 for "rb"? Actually t3 = 2 for read? Let me decode), a2 = mode (t5 = 420 = 0o644 octal). Then it malloc's a 16-byte FILE struct: [0]=fd, [4]=eof, [8]=error, [12]=position.

fread directly does syscall 0 (read) with fd from FILE->fd.

So the file I/O goes straight to the `syscall` instruction. The binary directly invokes `syscall` opcode in many places (not just syscall6), e.g., fopen uses inline `syscall` with v0=2 directly.

The fread implementation: `syscall` with v0 = t3 = 0 (read), a0 = fd, a1 = buf, a2 = count. Nice.

fseek, ftell, fwrite — likely also syscalls directly.

So our VM's syscall handler needs to handle:
- 0 read
- 1 write
- 2 open
- 3 close
- 8 lseek
- 60 exit
- 74 fsync (maybe)
- 82 rename
- 83 mkdir
- 87 unlink
- maybe others: gettimeofday? time? 

The time() and gettimeofday() in the binary are stubs (return 0). sleep/usleep may use syscalls? Let me check.

Also `I_Sleep` and timers in i_timer.c may use gettimeofday? The my_stdlib implementation might have its own gettimeofday stub returning 0ma. Let's check symbols table: gettimeofday at 0x43dc64 is just `jr ra; li v0,0` (returns 0). So I_GetTime uses "ticks" from DG_GetTicksMs (ms_ctr++)? Let me check i_timer.c / I_GetTime.

Also, printf goes through a syscall write. The printf implementation may need the full format-string formatting implemented in JS? No — printf is implemented in the binary (at 0x439938), using internal logic and syscalls to write. We don't need to implement printf in JS; the binary does formatting itself and just calls write syscall with a buffer and length. 

So syscall handler needs to handle actual syscalls.

Special instructions: bits 31:26 = 000000. Function = bits 5:0. There are many: sll, movz/movn, srl, sra, sllv, srlv, srav, jr, jalr, movz/movn... Also syscall, divu/div (0x1a/0x1b), mult, teq (traps?), etc.

RegImm: bits 31:26 = 000001: bltz, bgez, bltzal, bgezal, bgezal? and bltzal etc. MIPS r2.

So float usage in the actual boot path is minimal — v_video.c has `fabs(mouse_acceleration - 1)` but only when `usemouse` is true (probably false by default). Let me check D_DoomMain / I_InitVideo whether usemouse is set... Probably `usemouse` defaults false in this headless port. So floating point may not be exercised. Even if fabs is called, we could implement it in JS with plain Number since binary32 double conversion nuances may not matter for fabs. But `fabs` uses FPU instructions. If reachable, our interpreter must support mtc1/mov.d/c.olt.d/movt.d etc. That's very complex.

Alternatively, we can patch behavior: the FPU instructions are few; we could implement a "soft float" by emulating MIPS FP registers with JS numbers. But exact IEEE-754 binary32/binary64 behavior is needed for correctness though small rounding differences might be OK for fabs. However, implementing all FP ops (cvt, trunc, mul, add, div, neg, sqrt, comparisons) correctly is a lot.

Better approach: Prevent the FP code paths from being reached, or make the FP stubs return sane values. But the FP stubs are in the binary already (fabs, fabsf, sin, cos, sqrt). If we can implement just enough FPU instructions to support those stubs (mtc1, mthc1, mov.d, mov.s, c.olt.d, c.olt.s, movt.d, movt.s, neg.d, neg.s, cvt.s.w, cvt.d.s, trunc.w.s, add.s, mul.s, div.s, mfc1, etc.), that's manageable for a small subset (they're only 30 instructions total). Implementing correct binary32/binary64 semantics is a lot though.

Maybe we can wait and see: only if the VM hits FP instructions do we need them. If the boot path reaches FP (e.g., fabs), we handle it then. Let me first build the core integer VM and iterate.

Let me now think about the interpreter structure. I'll implement a straightforward fetch-decode-execute loop with a switch on opcode and funct. Registers stored in an array `regs[32]` (integers as JS numbers, but we must enforce 32-bit semantics with |0 where needed). The delay slot semantics: MIPS branches/jumps have delay slots. Must implement correctly! This is crucial for correctness.

Delay slot handling:
- For branches (beq, bne, blez, bgtz, bltz, bgez, etc.), the instruction after the branch is always executed (in the "delay slot"), then the branch happens. In the presence of `nop` in the delay slot (common), the effect is as expected.
- For jumps (j, jal), the delay slot also always executes.
- For `jal`, the link register ($31/ra) gets the address of the instruction after the delay slot (pc+8).
- For `jr`/`jalr`, delay slot also executes.

Implementation approach: In the fetch-decode-execute loop, when I encounter a control-flow instruction, I set the next PC, but first execute the instruction at PC+4 (the delay slot). However, the delay-slot instruction may itself be a branch/jump (rare). Standard approach: 
- Determine if the current instruction is a "branch or jump". If yes, we should execute the delay slot first)Skip. Then set PC = target (or for not-taken branches, PC+8).

But careful: for instructions in the delay slot that are themselves branches/jumps, the behavior gets complicated (the delay slot of a branch is executed before the branch; if the delay slot is itself a branch, that branch also has a delay slot). A common simplification: treat each branch/jump as: PC next = target; execute instruction at PC+4 first. If that instruction is a branch too, the infinite recursion... To avoid complexity, I'll implement the standard "assemble-time delay slot" by, upon a branch/jump instruction, first executing the instruction at PC+4 (with PC temporarily advanced), then jumping. Since real code mostly has `nop` in delay slots, and nested branches in delay slot are extremely rare (and don't occur in DOOM), this works.

Actually the cleanest correct implementation used by simple emulators:
```
for (;;) {
    insn = fetch(pc)
    // decode...
    if (is_branch_or_jump) {
        take = compute branch condition
        // execute delay slot
        exec(fetch(pc+4))  // but not counting this is complex
        pc = take ? target : pc+8
    } else {
        exec(insn)
        pc += 4
    }
}
```
But note some instructions read/write PC (e.g., jal sets ra). For `jal`, we must set ra = pc+8 regardless. And the delay slot executes.

However, some non-branch instructions also have "branch like" behavior: e.g., `movn`/`movz` are conditional moves (no delay slot). `syscall` no delay. `eret`/`wait` no.

There's also the special REGIMM branches where the delay slot executes, and the "branch likely" `bgezal`/`bltzal` which set link register $31 = pc+8.

Let me implement branch handling generically:
- I'll create a function `step()` that fetches word at pc, decodes, and executes.
- For branch/jump instructions, I'll first handle the "delay slot" by recursively executing the instruction at pc+4. But to keep it simple and correct for DOOM, I'll implement each branch by: compute target; execute delay slot instruction; then set pc = target if taken else pc+8.

The tricky part: When we execute the delay slot "recursively," that instruction runs with the current register state undexpected. This matches hardware (the delay slot executes with register values as they were before the branch, since the branch's effect on registers like ra happens after). Actually careful: for `jal`, the link register ra is set to pc+8 as part of the jal instruction; the delay slot executes before the jump but after ra is set? In hardware, `jal` sets ra to the address of the instruction after the delay slot (pc+8), and the delay slot executes normally. The register write to ra happens as part of the jal, and since the delay slot is a separate instruction after jal in program order, it could read ra... but normal code doesn't. I'll set ra in jal before executing delay slot. Similarly for `jalr`.

Let me now inspect what instructions need delay slots: j/jal/jr/jalr/beq/bne/blez/bgtz/beql?/bltz/bgez (REGIMM). This list from the disasm: j, jal, jalr, jr, beq/beqz, bne/bnez, blez, bgtz, bltz, bgez. Also `bc1t` (branch on FP condition) at? count 1 — FP branch, hopefully not reached.

Now the memory access functions: read 8/16/32 bits (big-endian? MIPS is byte-addressed; data is little-endian per ELF). MIPS is bi-endian but this binary is little-endian (ELF e_data = 1 = little). So multibyte loads/stores are little-endian. Implement:
- load8(addr): buf[addr-base]
- load16(addr): readUInt16LE
- load32(addr): readInt32LE (or readUInt32LE then to signed)
- store8, store16LE, store32LE

For unaligned accesses (LWL/LWR/SHL?) MIPS supports unaligned accesses naturally; Uint routines handle unaligned fine (Buffer readUInt32LE handles arbitrary offsets).

Address validity: all addresses within [0x400000, 0x80000000) (our single buffer). If a load/store is outside, throw error (or handle special regions like /dev/zero etc.? No, the guest won't).

Initial register state:
- $29 (sp) = stack top. Choose e.g. 0x7ff00000 (or 0x7fffdff0). Stack grows down (addiu sp,sp,-24). Let's set sp = 0x7fff0000 (16MB from 0x80000000). Actually let's compute: with base buffer starting 0x400000 and size 0x7fc00000 (ends at 0x80000000), set sp = 0x7fffe000 (near top). The guest immediately does addiu sp,sp,-24, then sw ra,20(sp). Stack usage downward.

Now, the syscall interface. The `syscall` instruction:
- v0 = syscall number
- a0-a3 = args 1-4
- t0, t1 = args 5-6 (for 6-arg syscalls, Linux MIPS uses more args: $a0-$a3, $t0, $t1).
- Return value in v0. Errors: negative errno returned in v0 (MIPS convention: negative). The binary's syscall6 wrapper checks v0<0 → v0=-1 and errno= -v0.

The syscalls we must implement:
- read(fd, buf, count)
- write(fd, buf, count)
- open(path, flags, mode)
- close(fd)
- lseek(fd, offset, whence)
- fsync(fd)
- rename(old,new)
- mkdir(path, mode)
- unlink(path)
- exit(code) — should terminate VM gracefully

Now the file I/O mapping: guest path strings are in guest memory. We need to read null-terminated strings from the guest bufferholarchy and translate to host paths.

FD table:
- 0, 1, 2: standard in/out/err → map to (stderr = process.stderr). Host: We'll attach write(1/2) to process.stdout/process.stderr (or console). For read(0), return 0 bytes / EAGAIN (no input).
- 3+: host file descriptors opened by us.

- Read guest path.

Now, the biggest risk: **Performance**. Running DOOM's boot in a JS interpreter. Let me estimate the instruction count before first frame. The boot does:
- Parse WAD (4.2MB) — reads via fread, sets up lump directory, hash table, checksums (SHA1 over WAD! that's heavy — SHA1 processes the whole 4.2MB in byte loops). SHA1 is a very heavy byte-by-byte loop requiring ~millions of ops per MB. In a JS interpreter, this could be extremely slow (e.g., 4.2MB * ~10 bytes/loop * ... maybe 100M+ interpreter steps). Could take minutes to hours. Hmm.

W_Checksum only hashes lump directory entries (not the WAD contents), and is only called in net code (D_ConnectNetGame). Still, SHA1_Final over numlumps entries (~1000 lumps) with ROL/ROR instructions — that's small (few thousand ops), not the whole 4.2MB. Good.

So boot computation is mostly:
- Reading 4.2MB WAD via fread (memcpy loops). The binary's memcpy is byte-by-byte (SB/LBU loops). That means copying 4.2MB byte-by-byte in the interpreter = ~4.2M iterations × several instructions each. That's maybe 50-200M interpreter steps. Could take a while in JS (maybe 30 sec - a few minutes). Hmm. But it's acceptable for the deliverable. We can optimize read syscall: the syscall handler reads from host file into guest memory directly via a Buffer copy (fast!). The WAD reading mostly uses fread → syscall read: our handler does mem.copy within Node Buffers — very fast. But then w_wad.c might do its own byte manipulations (e.g., converting lump names, etc.) which are modest.

Also, the rendering of the first title screen: draws 640x400 pixels by copying the TITLEPIC patch scaled. That's some loops but manageable.

Let me estimate more carefully: Bootstrap involves big loops in malloc init (4096 blocks), SHA1 over ~1000 lumps, WAD directory parsing (1000 lumps × small work), plus game init (sounds etc. — but sound is stubbed). Rendering TITLEPIC at 640x400 in 32-bit — V_DrawPatch scales 320x200→640x400 maybe, copying 256KB→1MB via per-pixel loops. That's ~256K loops. Fine.

The biggest potential performance killer: byte-by-byte memcpy of big data like the WAD file content into zone memory. The total might be ~10-20MB of memcpy. At say 5M instructions/sec (pessimistic), 20MB × ~5 insns = 100M insns = 20s. Realistically, an optimized JS interpreter can do 20-50M simple instructions/sec. So maybe 5-20 seconds. Acceptable.

Now let me design the VM in detail.

### ELF loading

```
const fs = require('fs');
const buf = fs.readFileSync('/app/doomgeneric_mips');
// ELF32 LE
const e_entry = buf.readUInt32LE(24);
const e_phoff = buf.readUInt32LE(28);
const e_phentsize = buf.readUInt16LE(42);
const e_phnum = buf.readUInt16LE(44);
```
Program headers at e_phoff, each 32 bytes:
- p_type (u32)
- p_offset (u32)
- p_vaddr (u32)
- p_paddr (u32)
- p_filesz (u32)
- p_memsz (u32)
- p_flags (u32)
- p_align (u32)

Collect PT_LOAD segments. Determine memory region min/max. Create memory Buffer covering [min, max) (probably 0x400000 to 0x80000000 including stack). But note: if we allocate buffer from 0x400000 with size covering up to 0x80000000, that's ~2GB. Let me instead do lazy paging to avoid the 2GB alloc being slow... Actually Buffer.alloc(2GB) on startup might take ~1-2 seconds; acceptable. But it also slows down with GC? Buffer is allocated from a slab; 2GB Buffer is a single slab — memory available. Let's test quickly how long a 2GB alloc takes.

Actually, let me be smarter: implement memory as a set of fixed pages (e.g., 64KB pages) lazily initialized. For speed, use a Buffer for the loaded segments and small buffer for stack. The guest rarely accesses the gap. Let me define:
- A function `mem` object with an array of pages; each page is a Buffer or a shared zero page. To keep loads fast, I'd rather have a single Buffer. Hmm.

Let me benchmark quickly in Node: allocate a 2GB Buffer, time it.

### Initial registers

- pc = e_entry (0x400110)
- regs[29] = sp = INITIAL_SP
- regs[28] = gp = 0 (or _gp; unused)
- regs[30] = fp = 0
- HI/LO registers for mult/div: $HI = regs[30], $LO = regs[31]? In MIPS, HI/LO are special (not GPRs). The `mfhi`/`mflo` instructions read HI/LO. Mult sets HI:LO. Div sets LO=quotient, HI=remainder. We'll store hi and lo as separate variables.

MIPS register mapping:
- $0 zero
- $1 at
- $2 v0
- $3 v1
- $4 a0
- $5 a1
- $6 a2
- $7 a3
- $8 t0
- $9 t1
- $10 t2
- $11 t3
- $12 t4
- $13 t5
- $14 t6
- $15 t7
- $16 s0
- $17 s1
- $18 s2
- $19 s3
- $20 s4
- $21 s5
- $22 s6
- $23 s7
- $24 t8
- $25 t9
- $26 k0
- $27 k1
- $28 gp
- $29 sp
- $30 fp/s8
- $31 ra

regs[0] must always be 0.

Registers as JS 32-bit signed ints. Use `|0` to emulate. For unsigned comparisons, store as signed but interpret via >>> 0 when needed.

### Instruction decoding

I'll write a compact decoder with a switch on opcode bits [31:26], then funct for SPECIAL/REGIMM/SPECIAL2/SIMM... Let me define per opcode.

#### I-type (opcode != 0,1,16,17,18,19,28,31... i.e., most):
- ADDI: rd = rs + sext(imm16)
- ADDIU: rd = rs + sext(imm16) (unsigned but same bit pattern)
- SLTI: rd = (rs < sext(imm16)) ? 1 : 0 (signed)
- SLTIU: rd = (rs_u < sext(imm16)_u) ? 1 : 0 (imm sign-extended then treated as unsigned)
- ANDI: rd = rs & zeroext(imm16)
- ORI: rd = rs | imm16
- XORI: rd = rs ^ imm16
- LUI: rd = imm16 << 16
- BEQ: if rs == rt → branch (delay slot, pc = target)
- BNE: if rs != rt → branch
- BLEZ: if rs <= 0 → branch (rt unused, must be 0 encoding)
- BGTZ: if rs > 0 → branch
- Also opcode 2 (J): pc = (pc+4)&0xf0000000 | (target<<2)
- opcode 3 (JAL): ra = pc+8; pc = ...
- opcode 1 (REGIMM), 8-15 as above.

#### Loads/Stores:
- LB (32): rt = signext8(mem[base+off])
- LBU (36): zeroext8
- LH (33): signext16
- LHU (37): zeroext16
- LW (35): 32-bit
- LWL (34): load word left — MIPS64-ish; in 32-bit, treat as LW? Need to check how objdump decoded LWL: probably used for unaligned? Actually LWL/LWR are part of MIPS III for 64-bit... In MIPS32, LWL (34) and LWR (38) don't exist? But objdump decoded opcodes 34 and 38 as LWL/LWR (20 each). And opcodes 42/46 as SWL/SWR (18 each). These are real MIPS64 instructions. For a 32-bit machine, they might be treated as LW/SW practically. In MIPS64 semantics, `lwl rt, offset(base)` loads the left 4 bytes of an 8-byte value and zeroes the right half? This is `P_RemoveMobj` — it reads a 9-byte structure (sprite name: 4+4+1 bytes: sprite, frame, etc.) from offset 143 and copies to another location with unaligned stores at offsets 0-8. This is LLVM's implementation of a 9-byte struct copy using LWL/LWR at byte offsets:
- `lwl at,143(s0)` reads 4 bytes at 143; `lwl v1,147(s0)` reads 4 bytes at 147; `lbu a2,148` reads byte 148.
- Then stores: `swl at,3(a1)`, `swl v1,7(a1)`, `swr v1,4(a1)`, `swr at,0(a1)`, `sb a2,8(a1)`.

So it blends halves of two words to copy 9 bytes unaligned. The semantics needed: LWL/LWR and SWL/SWR must produce/consume the right 32-bit halves.

In MIPS64 LWL/LWR (and SWL/SWR) semantics (from MIPS spec):
- `lwl rt, offset(base)`: The effective address is `base+offset`. Let A = aligned doubleword address (a&~7). LWL: loads the *left* (most significant) 32 bits from the aligned doubleword at A when the effective address is aligned, otherwise the right half...? Hmm actually:

MIPS III LWL/LWR definitions:
- `lwl`: Load Word Left: rt ← (the left half of the doubleword at aligned address) if the effective address is in the right half? 

Let me recall precisely from the MIPS spec:

For 64-bit GPRs, LWL/LWR load 32 bits into the left/right half of the 64-bit GPR. In 32-bit CPUs, Load Word Left/Right behavior:
- LWL: rt = memory[(align_down(ea, 8))] if ea mod 8 == 0? 

Hmm, honestly, I recall that in o32 (32-bit), compilers use LWL/LWR to load a 64-bit value (two words) from possibly unaligned address: `lwl rt, addr` loads the first (left) word, `lwr rt, addr` loads the second (right) word.

Interesting: `lwl at,143(s0)` then `lwr at,140(s0)` — both write `at`. The addresses differ by 3. In MIPS64 LWL/LWR, the effective address is masked to an aligned 8-byte boundary, so `lwl at,143(s0)` and `lwr at,140(s0)` both refer to the same aligned doubleword (140..147)! Yes: 140&~7 = 136? no. 140 & ~7 = 136, 8 bytes at 136-143. Hmm 143 is within 136..143. 140 is also within 136..143. So both use aligned address 136. Not the same though.

Wait 143 & ~7 = 136; 140 & ~7 = 136. Same aligned DE. LWL loads the left half (bytes at 136-139), LWR loads the right half (bytes at 140-143). But the struct member the code wants is at offset 143 (4 bytes) and 147 (4 bytes). Let's see how the store side works:

Let me think in terms of MIPS64 semantics: each GPR is 64-bit. LWL/LWR load into the left/right half of a 64-bit register. On a 32-bit machine these map to the 32-bit register. Actually for 32-bit GPRs, "left" and "right" halves don't exist; the practical o32 ABI behavior: LWL/LWR both load a 32-bit word, but from differently aligned addresses:
- LWL effective address handling: ea = base+offset; aligned = ea & ~7; LWL loads from aligned? or from aligned+4?

From actual MIPS III spec:
```
LWL rt, offset(base)
    vAddr = base + signext(offset)
    pAddr = vAddr & ~7
    if vAddr mod 8 >= 4:
        rt = mem[pAddr+4 .. pAddr+7]    (right half)
    else:
        rt = mem[pAddr.. pAddr+3]       (left half)
```
Hmm, I recall that LWL returns the word containing the effective address, but with the *right* half zeroed? I'm not certain.

```
ldl t0, offset(base)   # load left portion into low 32 bits? 
ldr t0, offset+4(base)? 
```

- LWL (Load Word Left): stores the data from the memory location at the effective address in the leftmost half of the register. The effective address is ANDed with a mask leaving the three least significant bits... In MIPS64: "The address is rounded down to an 8-byte boundary, and if the effective address was in the right half, it adds 4"? 

OK here's a cleaner way to reason: These instructions exist specifically so you can load/store misaligned 8-byte (or 4-byte at odd offsets) data. Consider the copy: destination pointer a1 = some address; the 9-byte copy writes bytes at offsets 0..8.

- LWL: Load Word Left. rt ← "memory[ea]" where ea = (base + offset) & ~7, and it loads the left or right half depending on alignment. The part loaded is the one *containing* the effective address, placed in the left half of the register?

I need the exact LWL/LWR semantics. Let me look at the MIPS spec logic:

From the R4000 spec:
```
LWL rt, offset(base)
    vAddr = base + sign_extend(offset)
    pAddr = vAddr & ~7
    if (vAddr & 4) != 0:   # effective address in right half
        rt = GPR[rt] << 32 | mem[pAddr+4, pAddr+8]   # store into low half
    else:
        rt = mem[pAddr, pAddr+4] << 32 | GPR[rt]      # store into high half
```

So LWL loads the 4-byte word containing the effective address into the LEFT (high) half of the 64-bit register, leaving the right half unchanged. LWR loads into the RIGHT (low) half.

On a 32-bit machine with only 32-bit registers, the semantics get reduced. The practical 32-bit behavior (as used by older MIPS compilers for unaligned 32-bit loads): 
- LWL at effective address: load the word at the aligned address (ea & ~3)? 

Hmm, let me think about the store pattern in the 9-byte copy to see if it forms a coherent memcpy:

Source: bytes S[143..151].
- at = LWL at 143: LWL means: load the 4 bytes of the 8-byte aligned doubleword containing 143... no wait.

Let me consider the actual aligned addresses:
- 143 → aligned 8 = 136; 143 is in the right half of 136..143 (since 143 >= 140). LWL loads the left half? no...

Ugh. Let me just find an authoritative description.

Actually, there's a simpler way: I can find where LLVM docs/tests describe the semantics. But no internet. Let me reason empirically from the byte-copy code structure.

The destination spans offsets 0..8 (9 bytes). The code writes:
- `swl at,3(a1)` and `swr at,0(a1)`: These two stores produce 8 consecutive bytes? SWL at offset 3 writes the "left half" and SWR at offset 0 writes "right half" such that together they write bytes 0..6 (with overlap at 3-6?). Actually if at is 64-bit, SWL stores the left 32 bits at the given address and SWR stores the right 32 bits. For a 64-bit register at, SWL at offset 3 would store at[63:32] to bytes 3..6, and SWR at 0 stores at[31:0] to bytes 0..3. Together they'd write bytes 0..6 (not 0..7). Hmm.

But if at is only 32-bit on a 32-bit machine, both store the 32-bit value. Then:
- swr at,0 → bytes 0..3 = at
- swl at,3 → bytes 3..6 = at  (overlaps byte 3!)
- swl v1,7 → bytes 7..10 = v1
- swr v1,4 → bytes 4..7 = v1 (overlaps byte 7)
- sb a2,8 → byte 8

With 32-bit at and v1, the bytes would be:
- byte0..3 = at
- byte3..6 = at (overwrites byte3 with at's byte0)
- byte4..7 = v1 (overwrites some)
- byte7..10 = v1
- byte 8 = a2

That's weird overlapping. So this suggests 64-bit semantics where SWL/SWR handle different halves.

In F_DrawPatchCol:
```
column = (column_t *)((byte *)patch + LONG(patch->columnofs[col]));
```
`patch->columnofs[col]` is a `int columnofs[8]` array with BIG-endian stored integers (LONG macro = swap endian). The code:
- `at = a1 + a2*4` → address of columnofs[col] within patch. a1 = patch pointer, a2 = col.
- `lwl v0,11(at)` — ? and `lwr v0,8(at)` — ? then `v1 = a1 + v0` → so v0 = column offset value (after endian swap).

The pattern `lwl v0,11(at)` and `lwr v0,8(at)` reads bytes at offsets 8..11 (4 bytes) — the columnofs entry at a1 + col*4 is at offset `at` which is `a1 + 4*col`; the data is at bytes offset 4*col + 8? Because patch_t header: columnofs starts at offset 8 in patch_t (after width, height, leftoffset, topoffset — 8 bytes). So columnofs[col] is at byte offset 8 + 4*col of patch. `at = a1 + 4*col`, so the entry is at `at + 8`. The instructions:
- `lwl v0,11(at)`: offset 11 (the +3 high offset?) 
- `lwr v0,8(at)`: offset 8.

So it loads a 32-bit value spanning bytes 8..11 — an unaligned (offset 8) load. LWL at offset 11 and LWR at offset 8 both write to v0)Skip. On a big-endian MIPS? Wait this is little-endian data. Actually WAD data is stored big-endian; `LONG()` swaps to host little-endian? Let me understand: `columnofs` in WAD is stored as big-endian signed 32-bit. The macro `LONG(x)` (from doomdef.h) converts from big-endian WAD to host endianness.

Hold on: In WAD files (DOOM), all multi-byte values are little-endian! Wait, actually DOOM WAD uses little-endian. Hmm, no — DOOM WAD format is little-endian (original PC format). `LONG()` in the source does `SWAP_INT32`? Let me check doomdef.h LONG macro.

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    277	// Helper function to find a file node by path
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    278	FileNode* find_node(const char* path, FileNode** parent_dir) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    279	    if (root == NULL) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    280	        init_filesystem();
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    281	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    282	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    283	    // Handle empty or root path
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    284	    if (path == NULL || path[0] == '\0' || strcmp(path, "/") == 0) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    285	        if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = NULL;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    286	        return root;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    287	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    288	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    289	    // Make a copy of the path to tokenize
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    290	    char path_copy[MAX_PATH_LENGTH];
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    291	    strncpy(path_copy, path, MAX_PATH_LENGTH);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    292	    path_copy[MAX_PATH_LENGTH - 1] = '\0';
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    293	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    294	    // Start at root
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    295	    FileNode* current = root;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    296	    FileNode* parent = NULL;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    297	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    298	    // Handle absolute path
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    299	    char* token = strtok(path_copy, "/");
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    300	    if (path[0] == '/') {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    301	        // Path starts with /, so we start at the root
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    302	        current = root;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    303	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    304	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    305	    // Traverse the path
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    306	    while (token != NULL) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    307	        if (current->type != FILE_TYPE_DIRECTORY) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    308	            return NULL; // Not a directory
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    309	        }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    310	        
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    311	        parent = current;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    312	        current = NULL;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    313	        
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    314	        // Look for the token in the current directory
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    315	        for (size_t i = 0; i < parent->num_entries; i++) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    316	            if (strcmp(parent->entries[i].name, token) == 0) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    317	                current = parent->entries[i].node;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    318	                break;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    319	            }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    320	        }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    321	        
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    322	        if (current == NULL) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    323	            // Not found
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    324	            if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = parent;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    325	            return NULL;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    326	        }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    327	        
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    328	        token = strtok(NULL, "/");
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    329	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    330	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    331	    if (parent_dir) *parent_dir = parent;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    332	    return current;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    333	}

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    365	// Helper function to add a node to a directory

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    401	// Helper function to allocate a file descriptor
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    402	int allocate_fd(FileNode* node, int mode) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    403	    for (int i = 0; i < MAX_FILE_DESCRIPTORS; i++) {

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    415	// Helper function to split a path into directory and filename
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    416	void split_path(const char* path, char* dir_path, char* filename) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    417	    const char* last_slash = strrchr(path, '/');
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    418	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    419	    if (last_slash == NULL) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    420	        // No directory part
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    421	        strcpy(dir_path, ".");
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    422	        strcpy(filename, path);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    423	    } else if (last_slash == path) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    424	        // Root directory
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    425	        strcpy(dir_path, "/");
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    426	        strcpy(filename, last_slash + 1);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    427	    } else {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    428	        // Extract directory path and filename
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    429	        size_t dir_len = last_slash - path;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    430	        strncpy(dir_path, path, dir_len);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    431	        dir_path[dir_len] = '\0';
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    432	        strcpy(filename, last_slash + 1);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    433	    }

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    947	// Main free with fast path for fixed-size pools

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    950	static unsigned char heap[HEAP_SIZE] __attribute__((aligned(64)));  // Cache line aligned
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    951	static size_t heap_pos = 0;  // Current position in the heap
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    952	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    953	// Size classes for segregated free lists

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    956	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    957	// Alignment
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    958	#define ALIGN_SIZE 8
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    959	#define ALIGN_MASK (ALIGN_SIZE - 1)
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    960	#define ALIGN_UP(size) (((size) + ALIGN_MASK) & ~ALIGN_MASK)
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    961	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    962	// Block header
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    963	typedef struct block_header {

[tool-1b3] read_file ->    965	} block_header_t;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    966	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    967	#define HEADER_SIZE ALIGN_UP(sizeof(block_header_t))
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    968	#define MIN_BLOCK_SIZE ALIGN_UP(HEADER_SIZE + sizeof(void*))  // Minimum size to store a free list pointer
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    969	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    970	// Get size class index for a given size
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    971	static int size_class_index(size_t size) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    972	    // For small sizes, use direct mapping to optimize common cases
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    973	  /*
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    974	    if (size <= 16) return 0;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    975	    if (size <= 32) return 1;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    976	    if (size <= 64) return 2;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    977	    if (size <= 128) return 3;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    978	    if (size <= 256) return 4;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    979	    if (size <= 512) return 5;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    980	    if (size <= 1024) return 6;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    981	  */
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    982	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    983	    // For larger sizes, use bit operations to find log2
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    984	    int index = 0;  // Start after direct mapping
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    985	    size >>= 4;    // Divide by 1024
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    986	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    987	    while (size) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    988	        size >>= 1;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    989	        index++;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    990	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    991	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    992	    return (index < NUM_SIZE_CLASSES) ? index : NUM_SIZE_CLASSES - 1;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    993	}
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    994	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    995	// Basic malloc implementation using segregated free lists
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    996	void* basic_malloc(size_t size) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    997	    if (size == 0) return NULL;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    998	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->    999	    // Calculate total size needed including header
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1000	    size_t total_size = ALIGN_UP(HEADER_SIZE + size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1001	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1002	    // Ensure minimum block size
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1003	    if (total_size < MIN_BLOCK_SIZE)
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1004	        total_size = MIN_BLOCK_SIZE;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1005	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1006	    // Get the appropriate size class
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1007	    int class_idx = size_class_index(total_size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1008	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1009	    //printf("malloc %d\n", size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1010	    // Try to find a free block in this or larger size classes
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1011	    for (int i = class_idx; i < NUM_SIZE_CLASSES; i++) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1012	        if (free_lists[i]) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1013	            // Found a free block, remove it from the list
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1014	            void* block_ptr = free_lists[i];
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1015	            block_header_t* block = (block_header_t*)((char*)block_ptr - HEADER_SIZE);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1016	            
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1017	            // Update free list to remove this block
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1018	            free_lists[i] = *((void**)block_ptr);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1019	            
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1020	            // Split if the block is significantly larger (only for larger blocks to avoid overhead)
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1021	            if (i > class_idx + 1 && block->size >= total_size + MIN_BLOCK_SIZE + HEADER_SIZE) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1022	                // Calculate sizes
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1023	                size_t remaining_size = block->size - total_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1024	                
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1025	                // Update current block size
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1026	                block->size = total_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1027	                
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1028	                // Create new block from remainder
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1029	                block_header_t* new_block = (block_header_t*)((char*)block + total_size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1030	                new_block->size = remaining_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1031	                
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1032	                // Add to appropriate free list
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1033	                int new_class = size_class_index(remaining_size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1034	                void* new_data = (char*)new_block + HEADER_SIZE;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1035	                *((void**)new_data) = free_lists[new_class];
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1036	                free_lists[new_class] = new_data;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1037	            }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1038	            
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1039	            // Return the data portion of the block
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1040	            return block_ptr;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1041	        }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1042	    }
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1043	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1044	    // No free block found, allocate from the heap
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1045	    if (heap_pos + total_size > HEAP_SIZE)
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1046	        return NULL;  // Out of memory
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1047	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1048	    // Create a new block at the current heap position
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1049	    block_header_t* new_block = (block_header_t*)(heap + heap_pos);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1050	    new_block->size = total_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1051	    heap_pos += total_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1052	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1053	    // Return the data portion of the block
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1054	    return (char*)new_block + HEADER_SIZE;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1055	}
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1056	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1057	// Simple and fast free implementation
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1058	void basic_free(void* ptr) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1059	    if (!ptr) return;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1060	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1061	    // Get the block header
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1062	    block_header_t* block = (block_header_t*)((char*)ptr - HEADER_SIZE);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1063	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1064	    // Validate the pointer
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1065	    if ((void*)block < (void*)heap || (void*)block >= (void*)(heap + heap_pos))
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1066	        return;  // Invalid pointer
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1067	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1068	    // Add to appropriate free list based on size
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1069	    int class_idx = size_class_index(block->size);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1070	    *((void**)ptr) = free_lists[class_idx];
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1071	    free_lists[class_idx] = ptr;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1072	}
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1073	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1074	// Optional: Faster implementation for small, fixed-size allocations
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1075	#define NUM_POOLS 4                        // Number of pools for common sizes
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1076	#define POOL_SIZES {16, 32, 64, 128}      // Common allocation sizes
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1077	#define POOL_CHUNK_COUNT 1024             // Chunks per pool
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1078	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1079	static struct {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1080	    void* free_chunks;                     // Free chunk list
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1081	    unsigned char* memory;                 // Pool memory
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1082	    size_t chunk_size;                     // Size of each chunk
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1083	    size_t used_count;                     // Number of chunks used
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1084	} fixed_pools[NUM_POOLS];
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1085	
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1086	// Initialize fixed-size pools for common allocations
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1087	static void init_fixed_pools() {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1088	    static int initialized = 0;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1089	    if (initialized) return;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1090	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1091	    const size_t sizes[NUM_POOLS] = POOL_SIZES;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1092	    
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1093	    for (int i = 0; i < NUM_POOLS; i++) {
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1094	        size_t chunk_size = ALIGN_UP(sizes[i]);
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1095	        size_t pool_size = POOL_CHUNK_COUNT * chunk_size;
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1096	        
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1097	        // Allocate pool from the heap
[tool-1b3] read_file ->   1098	        fixed_pools[i].memory = heap + heap_pos;

Now I understand the stdlib. The important aspect: the MIPS binary makes real Linux syscalls via inline asm (`syscall` instruction with numbers in v0/a0..). So the emulator must handle the `syscall` instruction (opcode 0x0000000c) and implement the needed Linux syscalls on MIPS o32 ABI:
- SYS_read=4003 (MIPS o32 read is 4003!) NOT 0. Wait. The my_stdlib.c defines `SYS_read 0`, `SYS_write 1`, etc. for x86_64. But then there's a `real_syscall6` for MIPS that does `syscall` instruction passing syscall_num in v0. These SYS_* numbers used in the code are x86_64 numbers! Hold on. Let me re-read.

In the MIPS branch of real_syscall6, the inline asm just does `syscall` with whatever number is in %1 (syscall_num). The caller passes e.g. `SYS_write` which is defined as 1. So on MIPS, the actual syscall numbers used are the x86_64 numbers (1 for write, 0 for read, 2 for open, 3 for close, 8 for lseek, 60 for exit, 201 for time, 96 gettimeofday, 35 nanosleep, 74 fsync, 82 rename, 83 mkdir, 87 unlink).

So this is a quirk: the code passes x86_64 syscall numbers to MIPS `syscall` instructionainer. On real Linux, MIPS syscall number 1 = exit, not write! But this is running inside the emulator; the emulator can map the numbers as the code intends them (x86-64 numbers). So the emulator should interpret syscall numbers as given by the program: 0=read, 1=write, 2=open, 3=close, 8=lseek, 60=exit, 201=time, 96=gettimeofday, 35=nanosleep, 74=fsync, 82=rename, 83=mkdir, 87=unlink.

Actually let me search where real_syscall6 is invoked. We need to know exactly which syscalls DOOM will make. Let's look at the whole my_stdlib.c to see where syscall6/real_syscall6 are usedcones. From the code:

- `real_syscall6(SYS_open...)` in init_filesystem() - but that's inside `#if 0` block! Lines 117-912 are `#if 0`...`#endif`. So `syscall_fs`, `sys_read`, `sys_write`, `sys_open`, etc. are all compiled OUT. The fake filesystem is disabled; `USE_FS` is not defined beenSince.

- `syscall6` is used for `syscall3(SYS_write, ...)` in putchar, fwrite, fread, fopen, etc. Those are real (after #endif). syscall6 calls real_syscall6 for write to fd 0/1. For file-related syscalls, the switch inside syscall6 returns nothing (0) because USE_FS is not defined — wait, look:

```c
static long syscall6(...) {
    ...
    if (syscall_num == SYS_write && (arg1 == 0 || arg1 == 1)) {
      return real_syscall6(...);
    }

    /* Check if this is a file-related syscall that should be handled by fake_fs.c */
    switch (syscall_num) {
        case SYS_read:
        case SYS_write:
        case SYS_open:
        case SYS_close:
        case SYS_lseek:
        case SYS_fsync:
        case SYS_rename:
        case SYS_mkdir:
        case SYS_unlink:
        #ifdef USE_FS
        return syscall_fs(...);
        #else
        0;
        #endif
    }

    return real_syscall6(syscall_num, arg1, arg2, arg3, arg4, arg5, arg6);
}
```

If USE_FS is not defined, the switch cases fall through to the final `return real_syscall6(...)`. So all file operations (open/write/read/lseek/close) go to the real Linux syscalls using x86_64 numbers. So the emulator needs to handle the Linux x86-64 syscall numbers as used on MIPS. This confirms we should implement the syscalls with the *numbers as the program uses them* (0 read, 1 write, 2 open, 3 close, 8 lseek, 60 exit, 201 time, 96 gettimeofday, 74 fsync, 82 rename, 83 mkdir, 87 unlink).

[tool-23f] read_file ->    392	static boolean DirIsFile(char *path, char *filename)
[tool-23f] read_file ->    393	{
[tool-23f] read_file ->    394	    size_t path_len;
[tool-23f] read_file ->    395	    size_t filename_len;

[tool-23f] read_file ->    398	    filename_len = strlen(filename);
[tool-23f] read_file ->    399	
[tool-23f] read_file ->    400	    return path_len >= filename_len + 1
[tool-23f] read_file ->    401	        && path[path_len - filename_len - 1] == DIR_SEPARATOR
[tool-23f] read_file ->    402	        && !strcasecmp(&path[path_len - filename_len], filename);
[tool-23f] read_file ->    403	}

Let me enumerate the MIPS instructions I must implement:
- LUI, ORI, ADDIU, ADDI, ANDI, XORI, XOR, AND, OR, NOR, SLTI, SLTIU, SLT, SLTU

- SLL, SRL, SRA, SLLV, SRLV, SRAV

Actually MADD/MADDU are defined in MIPS-3D / MDMX? Hmm, actually MIPS32r2 spec includes MADD, MADDU, MSUB, MSUBU? Let me not guess; I'll enumerate opcodes actually present in the binary.)
- 64-bit ops: MFLO/MFHI (for 64-bit multiply accumulate), or the compiler may use mult/multu + mfhi/mflo sequence to implement 64-bit multiply. Actually the classic MIPS pattern for 64-bit multiply: `mfhi`/`mflo` pair with `multu` produces 64-bit result in hi/lo registers. I need to support MULT, MULTU, DIV, DIVU (32x32→32), plus the special "HI/LO" registers and MFHI/MFLO instructions.

- Branch/Jumps: BEQ, BNE, BLEZ, BGTZ, BLTZ, BGEZ, (with branch likely target forms), J, JAL, JR, JALR; delay slots (branch delay slot).

- Loads/stores: LB (LBU), LBU (lbu = load byte unsigned — MIPS o32 has "lbu" opcode 0x90...; the disasm shows `lbu at,20708(at)`, so LBU = load byte unsigned, 0x90), LH (LHU = load halfword unsigned), LW, LWL, LWR, SB, SH, SW, SWL, SWR. Also unaligned loads/stores may occur.

- Moves: MOVN/MOVZ (conditional moves — "movn" seen in disasm at 0x4001ac: `movn v0,v1,at`).

- SYSBREAK 0x0000000c (syscall).
- FLOATING POINT: fpu ops like LWC1/SWC1, ADD.S, SUB.S, MUL.S, DIV.S, ABS.S, C.LE.S, C.LT.S, C.EQ.S, TRUNC.W.S, CVT.W.S, MOV.S, NEG.S, etc. Does DOOM use floating point? [tool-1bd] exec_command <- Enumerate instruction classes present in binary ($ cat > /tmp/enum.py <<'EOF' import struct, collections d=open('/app/doomgeneric/doomgeneric/doomgeneric_mips','rb').read() base=0x400110; text_off=0x110; text_size=0x3dbd0 # Load .text words words=[]; addrs=[] for off in range(text_off, text_off+text_size, 4): words.append(struct.unpack_from('<I',d,off)[0]); addrs.append(base+(off-text_off)) # classify top-level opcode field top=collections.Counter(w>>26 for w in words) print("opcode field counts (top 6 bits):") for k,v in top.most_common(): print(f" {k:3d} {v:6d}") print("\nopcode 0 (SPECIAL) funct counts:") c0=collections.Counter((w>>0)&0x3f for w in words if (w>>26)==0) for k,v in sorted(c0.items()): print(f" funct={k:2d} {v:6d}") print("\nopcode 17 (COP1) funct counts (MIPS32 r2 FP):") c17=collections.Counter((w>>0)&0x3f for w in words if (w>>26)==17) for k,v in sorted(c17.items()): print(f" funct={k:2d} {v:6d}") print("\nopcode 0x31 (LWC1) / 0x39 (LDC1) / 0x39 etc counters:") print(collections.Counter((w>>26) for w in words if (w>>26) in (0x31,0x39,0x3a,0x3b,0x32,0x3e,0x3f))) # REGIMM (1) rt values print("\nopcode 1 (REGIMM) rt counts:") c1=collections.Counter((w>>16)&0x1f for w in words if (w>>26)==1) for k,v in sorted(c1.items()): print(f" rt={k:2d} {v:6d}") print("\nopcode 2/3 (j/jal) targets format fields:") c23=collections.Counter((w>>26) for w in words if (w>>26) in (2,3)) print(c23) # Other opcodes print("\nother opcodes:") for k in sorted(top): if k not in (0,1,2,3,17,0x31,0x39): print(f" {k} ({k:05b}): {top[k]}") EOF python3 /tmp/enum.py)

Let me write a decoder using an instruction table. I'll define the MIPS32 ISA based on the encoding types:

Bit layout:
- op = (word>>26)&0x3F
Register fields:
- rs = (word>>21)&0x1F
- rt = (word>>16)&0x1F
- rd = (word>>11)&0x1F
- sa/shamt = (word>>6)&0x1F
- funct = word & 0x3F
- imm = word & 0xFFFF
- target = word & 0x03FFFFFF

opcode types:
- 0: SPECIAL
- 1: REGIMM (BLTZ/BGEZ/BLTZAL/BGEZAL, rt=0/1)
- 2: J
- 3: JAL
- 4: BEQ
- 5: BNE
- 6: BLEZ
- 7: BGTZ
- 8: [ADDI]? No wait: 8 ADDI, 9 ADDIU, 10 SLTI, 11 SLTIU, 12 ANDI, 13 ORI, 14 XORI, 15 LUI
- 16: MTC0? no — 16 is... Actually opcode 16 = 0x10 = "COP0"/"MTC0"? On MIPS32 user mode? Hmm 16 is special2? Let me recall the MIPS32 opcode map:
  - 0 SPECIAL
  - 1 REGIMM
  - 2 J
  - 3 JAL
  - 4 BEQ
  - 5 BNE
  - 6 BLEZ
  - 7 BGTZ
  - 8 ADDI
  - 9 ADDIU
  - 10 SLTI
  - 11 SLTIU
  - 12 ANDI
  - 13 ORI
  - 14 XORI
  - 15 LUI
  - 16 MTC0 (Cop0)/? On MIPS32: opcode 16 = "MTC0" (cop0 move to control)? Actually MTC0 = opcode 16 (0x10) per spec: "MTC0" is 010000. Yes.
  - 17 COP1 (FPU & mtc1/mfc1)
  - 18 COP0 (was "BC0F"?)
  - 19 COP2 (unused in most)
  - 20 BEQL, 21 BNEL, 22 BLEZL, 23 BGTZL, 24 DADDI, 25 DADDIU, 26 DSLTI, 27 DSLTIU, 28 DANDI, 29 DORI, 30 DXORI, 31 DLUI (these are cop2/octeon? not needed)
  - 32 LB (0x20)
  - 33 LH (0x21)
  - 34 LWL (0x22)
  - 35 LW (0x23)
  - 36 LBU (0x24)
  - 37 LHU (0x25)
  - 38 LWR (0x26)
  - 39 LDC1? no — opcode 39 = 0x27 = "LDL"? For R5900 LDL. Then 40-43 = cop1 LD? Hmm.
  - 40 SB (0x28)
  - 41 SH (0x29)
  - 42 SWL (0x2a)
  - 43 SW (0x2b)
  - 44 SDL?
  - 45 SDR?
  - 46 SWR (0x2e)
  - 47 CACHE (0x2f)
  - 49 (0x31) COP1 LWC1 → objdump "lwc1"/"ldc1" (the mnemonics `lwc1` 5 and `sdc1` 2, `ldc1` 2)
  - 61 (0x3d) COP1? — "c.olt.d" etc.
  - 53 (0x35) = COP1? cvt.d.s etc.

[tool-e59] exec_command <- Decode rare opcodes ($ cat > /tmp/decode.py <<'EOF' import struct d=open('/app/doomgeneric/doomgeneric/doomgeneric_mips','rb').read() base=0x400110; text_off=0x110; text_size=0x3dbd0 def fields(w): op=(w>>26)&0x3f; rs=(w>>21)&0x1f; rt=(w>>16)&0x1f; rd=(w>>11)&0x1f sa=(w>>6)&0x1f; funct=w&0x3f; imm=w&0xffff; tgt=w&0x03ffffff return op,rs,rt,rd,sa,funct,imm,tgt # Map opcode -> name for known opname={0:'SPECIAL',1:'REGIMM',2:'J',3:'JAL',4:'BEQ',5:'BNE',6:'BLEZ',7:'BGTZ', 8:'ADDI',9:'ADDIU',10:'SLTI',11:'SLTIU',12:'ANDI',13:'ORI',14:'XORI',15:'LUI', 16:'MTC0',17:'COP1',18:'COP0',19:'COP2', 20:'BEQL',21:'BNEL',22:'BLEZL',23:'BGTZL',24:'DADDI',25:'DADDIU',26:'DSLTI',27:'DSLTIU',28:'DANDI',29:'DORI',30:'DXORI',31:'DLUI', 32:'LB',33:'LH',34:'LWL',35:'LW',36:'LBU',37:'LHU',38:'LWR',39:'LDL', 40:'SB',41:'SH',42:'SWL',43:'SW',44:'SDL',45:'SDR',46:'SWR',47:'CACHE', 48:'LL',49:'LWC1',50:'LWC1',51:'LWC1',52:'LDC1',53:'LDC1',54:'LDC1',55:'LDC1', 56:'LQC2',57:'LDC1',58:'LDC1',59:'LDC1',60:'SPC1',61:'SPC1',62:'LDC1',63:'LDC1' } # Examine unusual opcodes import collections seen=collections.defaultdict(list) for off in range(text_off, text_off+text_size, 4): w=struct.unpack_from('<I',d,off)[0] op,rs,rt,rd,sa,funct,imm,tgt=fields(w) seen[op].append((w,off)) # print samples of rare opcodes for op in [16,18,19,28,31,39,44,45,47,48,49,53,61]: if op in seen: print(f"== opcode {op} name={opname.get(op,'?')} count={len(seen[op])}") for w,off in seen[op][:4]: addr=base+(off-text_off) print(f" {addr:08x}: {w:08x} op={op} rs={fields(w)[1]} rt={fields(w)[2]} rd={fields(w)[3]} sa={fields(w)[4]} funct={fields(w)[5]} imm={fields(w)[6]:04x}") print() print("== SPECIAL funct samples") for ft in [16,17,18,24,25,26,27,28,29,30,31,33,35,36,37,38,39,42,43,52]: cnt=0 for off in range(text_off, text_off+text_size, 4): w=struct.unpack_from('<I',d,off)[0] if (w>>26)==0 and (w&0x3f)==ft: if cnt<3: addr=base+(off-text_off) print(f" {addr:08x}: {w:08x}") cnt+=1 print(f" funct {ft}: total {cnt}") EOF python3 /tmp/decode.py)

[tool-70a] exec_command <- List distinct instruction encodings with objdump mnemonics ($ cat > /tmp/uniq.py <<'EOF' import re, struct, collections # parse disasm: lines like " 400110:\t27bdffe8\taddiu\tsp,sp,-24" info={} # addr -> (hex, mnemonic, operands) lastfunc=None for line in open('/tmp/disasm.txt'): m=re.match(r'\s*([0-9a-fA-F]+):\t([0-9a-fA-F]{8})\t([a-z0-9.]+)(.*)$', line) if m: addr=int(m.group(1),16); hx=m.group(2); mn=m.group(3); ops=m.group(4).strip() info[addr]=(hx,mn,ops) d=open('/app/doomgeneric/doomgeneric/doomgeneric_mips','rb').read() base=0x400110; text_off=0x110; text_size=0x3dbd0 enc=collections.defaultdict(list) for off in range(text_off, text_off+text_size, 4): w=struct.unpack_from('<I',d,off)[0] addr=base+(off-text_off) if addr in info: hx,mn,ops=info[addr] enc[(mn,hx)].append(addr) else: enc[('??',f'{w:08x}')].append(addr) print("total distinct encodings:", len(enc)) for (mn,hx),addrs in sorted(enc.items(), key=lambda kv: kv[0][1]): print(f"{mn:12s} {hx} x{len(addrs):5d} e.g. {addrs[0]:08x}") EOF python3 /tmp/uniq.py)
